利率上行引發波動，惟無損 AI 供應鏈擴張動能
2026 年 10 月
9 月全球股市普遍回落，主因為油價急升、全球長端殖利率攀高及主要央行轉鷹。僅有日本股市
受 AI、半導體需求與企業信心支撐，抵銷 BOJ 升息壓力，成為主要市場中唯一正報酬。標普 500 9
月小跌 0.3%，雖 AI 與大型科技股獲利韌性仍在，但 Fed 於 9 月升息並暗示年底前再度升息，加上
油價與美債殖利率走高，壓抑估值並引發科技股震盪。印度股市下跌 6.9%最弱，主要受油價飆升、
全球利率上行及外資單月淨流出約 27 億美元拖累。中國股市下跌 4.7%，因受內需修復偏弱、科技

股評價承壓所致；歐洲股市也因油價單月大漲近 14%與全球殖利率攀升影響，9 月下跌 4.6%。
9 月主要債市全面下跌，核心壓力來自全球殖利率急升。美歐日央行同步升息、Brent 原油單月
上漲約 14%、 美國經濟數據偏強與公債供給疑慮 ， 使美國10 年期殖利率單月上升約 53bp，創 2022
年以來最大升幅。主要券種中，全球投資級公司債下跌 2.93%表現最弱，新興美元債亦下跌 2.91%，
因 9 月全球公債殖利率快速攀升，此兩類債券對利率變化均較敏感 ； 其中投資級公司債相較高收益債
存續期較長、票息緩衝較低，因此在利率重新定價期間承受較大價格壓力。亞洲高收益債下挫 1.20%
相對抗跌，因高票息與較短存續期提供緩衝；新興當地債跌 1.74%，亦優於多數美元債種。
在股市方面，AI 基礎建設需求持續擴張，帶動 HBM、伺服器 DRAM、先進製程與封裝等關鍵供
應鏈維持成長動能，市場表現由全面上漲逐步轉向結構性分化。若AI 伺服器需求持續成長且 CSP 資
本支出維持高檔，具供給優勢、定價能力及較高訂單能見度之企業，其營運表現有機會獲得支撐。整

體而言 ， 科技產業與台灣及日本股市獲利表現仍具關注度 ， 惟後續仍須留意全球景氣 、 產業供需變化 、
政策監管及市場評價調整等風險因素。
就債市而言，雖然貨幣政策風險下降有助於穩定長期通膨預期，但政府與企業發債供給增加所帶
來的排擠效應，以及美國經濟成長展現韌性，仍可能使長天期利率面臨上行壓力。因此，在當前利率
環境下，中天期債券於票息收益與存續期間風險之間相對較為均衡。投資級債方面，彭博美國投資級
公司債指數利差約 80 個基點 ， 雖然利差偏窄 ， 但高基準利率環境仍提供相對較高的收益水準 。 因此，
在目前市場環境下 ， 中天期美國公債及投資級債仍為值得持續觀察的債券資產類別 ， 惟後續表現仍須
留意利率走勢、信用利差變化及經濟成長動能等因素。

美國股市：通膨與升息預期上升推動美債利率上漲，美股呈高檔區間震盪
9 月因美伊關係緊張升溫 ， 推升油價並加劇市場對通膨回升的擔憂 ；相較於 6 月點陣圖 ，9 月FOMC
官員全面上修 2026 至 2027 年的政策利率預估至 4.1%，並暗示 2026 年底前會再升息一次，加上美
國經濟數據表現優於預期，使美國公債殖利率走升，進而壓抑美股表現。在此背景下，9 月標普 500
指數呈現高檔區間震盪格局。產業表現方面，資訊科技與通訊服務類股相對強勢，持續扮演指數上漲
的主要動能；相較之下，醫療保健、必需消費及部分工業類股表現較為疲弱。

9 月美國聯準會貨幣政策會議以 12 比 0 的表決結果 ， 將聯邦基金利率目標區間上調25 個基點至
3.75%到 4.00%。9 月最新經濟預測，美國聯準會官員上修 2026 年核心 PCE 至 3.4%，2027 年維持
在 2.5%，將 2026-2027 年經濟成長上調 0.1%至 2.3%與 2.4%，顯示聯準會認為美國經濟與通膨仍
具韌性。
觀察美國近期的經濟數據可以發現企業投資動能依然強健，元大投顧表示，美國8 月扣除飛機與
國防的核心資本財訂單由前期的月增 0.6%加速至 1.6%， 其中初級金屬 、 機械及電機設備訂單均呈現
增長，顯示 AI 相關投資及企業設備更新需求持續支撐資本財訂單，企業設備投資可望對經濟成長形
成穩健貢獻。
自 6 月底以來 ， 市場持續上修標普500 指數企業獲利預估 ， 其中以科技與能源類股貢獻最為明顯 ，
反映企業營運表現優於市場預期。AI 投資循環持續推進、企業數位轉型需求增加，以及美國內需維
持穩健，共同支撐企業獲利成長動能，並有助於吸收高利率環境帶來的壓力。與此同時，標普 500

指數前瞻本益比已由先前高點回落，評價壓力較先前明顯緩解。隨著企業獲利持續改善，美股行情正
由過去仰賴資金與情緒推升評價 ， 逐步轉向由基本面支撐股價 ， 後續表現將更取決於企業獲利是否持
續優於預期。
投資策略方面，投資焦點已逐漸由評價擴張轉向企業獲利成長與基本面表現。短期市場仍須留意
政策面不確定性 ， 美中貿易談判進展將牽動全球供應鏈與科技產業展望 ， 若關稅或科技限制措施再度
升溫，可能對科技股評價造成壓力；此外，美國期中選舉逐漸接近，企業稅率、科技監管及產業政策
等議題亦可能影響市場風險偏好。整體而言，企業獲利改善仍是支撐美股最重要的力量，若 AI 投資
循環與獲利成長趨勢延續，美股中長期表現仍可望由基本面驅動。

歐洲股市：儘管企業獲利上修，仍需持續關注地緣政治發展
9 月泛歐 600 指數下跌約 2%，產業表現方面 ，製藥 、生技、銀行及部分科技類股表現相對強勢，
顯示資金偏好具獲利能見度與防禦特性的族群 ； 相較之下 ， 礦業 、 消費及部分金融服務類股表現落後 ，
反映市場對歐洲經濟成長及企業獲利前景仍持審慎態度。整體而言，受到高油價以及高利率環境，投
資氣氛轉趨保守，市場資金持續聚焦於基本面較穩健的產業。
歐洲央行 9 月政策利率由 2.25% 升息 1 碼至 2.50 % ，符合市場預期。歐洲央行最新經濟預
測中上修中期通膨展望，2027 年消費者物價指數年增率由 2.3% 升至 2.5% ，2028 年則由 2.0%

上調至 2.1% ；核心消費者物價指數方面，2027 年和 2028 年預測亦各上修 0.1 % 至 2.6% 及
2.3% ，能源成本上升對食品及其他價格的傳導，使得通膨回落速度較原先預期緩慢。ECB 總裁拉加
德表示能源價格上漲逐步傳導至核心及食品通膨，預期整體通膨在 2027 年上半年維持在高檔。
根據花旗歐洲獲利修正指數(Earnings Revision Index) ，歐洲企業獲利預估已連續上修並到近五
年高點，反映企業基本面持續改善。受惠於歐元區景氣穩步復甦、製造業景氣回升，以及企業資本支
出逐步回暖，工業、金融及景氣循環類股獲利展望同步改善。隨著市場對企業未來盈餘預期提高，有
助支撐歐股評價面與資金流入，並成為推動泛歐 600 指數表現的重要動能。
然而，自 3 月美伊衝突爆發以來，市場對能源供給中斷的擔憂持續升溫，帶動油價走高，進而增
加仰賴進口能源的歐洲企業成本壓力，並壓抑歐股相對表現。進入9 月後，俄羅斯與北約緊張關係再
度升溫，歐洲安全局勢不確定性提高，進一步加劇市場對能源供應與地緣政治風險的擔憂。若相關衝

突持續延燒，不僅可能推升能源價格與通膨壓力，也可能影響企業投資意願及市場風險偏好，進而對
歐洲企業獲利與股市表現形成壓力。
展望 10 月，歐股仍將受到高油價、地緣政治風險及歐洲央行維持偏鷹立場等因素影響，市場波
動性可能維持偏高。近期能源價格攀升推升通膨預期，使 ECB 傾向維持較高利率水準，不僅提高企
業融資成本，也限制股市評價進一步擴張的空間。此外，中東局勢持續緊張，以及俄羅斯與北約間的
緊張關係再度升高，均可能影響投資人風險偏好，增添歐洲金融市場不確定性。雖然歐元區經濟活動
仍維持溫和擴張，企業獲利預估亦持續改善，但在高利率與地緣政治風險尚未明顯緩解之前，市場情
緒仍偏向審慎，後續仍需持續關注能源價格、通膨走勢及地緣政治發展，作為判斷歐股能否重啟上漲
動能的重要觀察指標。

日本股市：升息落地，景氣展望佳，第四季半導體與商社企業獲利動能有望延續
9 月日本股市受升息預期及長天期公債殖利率走高影響，部分高評價股票出現震盪，但這波調整
較偏向政策轉折下的評價重估，而非企業基本面全面轉弱。日本銀行於 9 月 18 日決議將短期政策利
率目標提高至約 1.25%，使市場反覆討論的升息方向逐漸明朗。隨著政策落地，投資焦點有望由利率
變動轉回企業營運與獲利。從正面角度看，利率正常化反映日本經濟具備一定韌性，股價整理也有助
於消化先前累積的評價壓力。若後續企業財測保持穩健，9 月的震盪可望成為第四季行情重新蓄勢的

過程。
日本股市的中期支撐，來自薪資成長、內需改善與企業投資的相互帶動。2026 年春鬥最終統計
顯示，5,368 個工會的平均調薪幅度達 5.01%，連續第三年維持在 5%以上；可明確辨識的基本薪資
調升幅度亦達 3.50%。薪資持續增加，有助於提升家庭消費能力，為零售與服務等內需產業提供較穩
定的需求基礎。若企業能透過產品競爭力、定價能力及經營效率吸收成本，消費回溫便有機會轉化為
獲利成長。同時，全球 AI 相關投資也為日本半導體設備、載板及電子零組件供應鏈創造機會，使內
需復甦與科技需求形成雙重支撐。
展望 10 月，隨著升息議題逐步由預期轉為已知條件，市場可望更加重視第四季訂單、企業財測
及實際獲利。科技產業方面，若全球 AI 相關資本支出持續擴大，日本具技術優勢的設備與零組件企
業，有機會將需求轉化為接單及出貨成長；商社與資源相關企業，則可觀察能源、原物料業務及投資
收益對現金流的貢獻。內需產業亦值得留意薪資成長能否帶動消費，以及企業能否維持利潤率。整體
而言，10 月的投資機會不必侷限於單一題材，市場有望逐步從政策消息主導，轉向由產業需求與企

業業績支撐的行情。
投資策略方面，半導體供應鏈可優先挑選訂單能見度高、具技術門檻且獲利成長明確的企業；商
社則著重現金流、資本配置與股東回饋，內需股則觀察消費回溫能否反映在營收及利潤。若股價因利
率或匯率波動而短暫回落，但企業基本面與財測未明顯改變，可考慮分批布局，避免追逐短線漲幅。
後續仍須追蹤長天期利率 、 日圓及企業財測變化 ， 但投資主軸可更積極地放在具備實質成長能力的公
司。日本央行升息落地後，企業獲利能否接棒，將是日本股市延續表現的關鍵。

台灣股市：訂單持續暢旺突破千億，AI 需求持續向供應鏈擴散，廠商展望樂觀
9 月觀察台股基本面，最明確的支撐來自 8 月外銷訂單創下單月新高，以及企業對 9 月接單維持
擴張預期。經濟部公布，8 月外銷訂單達 1,029.6 億美元，月增 5.1%、年增 71.4%；其中資訊通信
與電子產品合計 799.6 億美元，占整體 77.7%，顯示 AI 伺服器、半導體與雲端建設仍是主要成長來
源。受查廠商對 9 月接單的預期，以訂單金額計算的動向指數為 52.1，高於代表持平的 50，意味整
體接單有望較 8 月增加。雖然 9 月實際結果仍待公布，但已發布的數據與企業展望，為台股進入第

四季提供了正面的基本面依據。
更值得留意的是，AI 需求正從核心晶片延伸至更廣的供應鏈。雲端業者擴充運算能力，除了帶動
伺服器與半導體接單，也使自研 ASIC、IC 設計及相關電子零組件成為後續觀察重點；半導體投資與
設備擴產 ， 則為自動化設備 、 銅箔及高階材料創造機會 。8 月機械與基本金屬外銷訂單分別年增 28.3%
及 26.8%，顯示科技投資的效益已反映在部分非電子貨品。對台股而言，這有助於投資主線從少數大
型權值股，延伸至具技術優勢與訂單能見度的中上游企業。不過，面板需求仍偏弱，產業表現並非全
面同步，選股仍須辨別企業實際受惠程度。
展望 10 月，市場焦點可望轉向第三季財報、第四季營運展望，以及 9 月外銷訂單能否驗證接單
續強。就廠商對 9 月的預期而言，資訊通信與電子產品的訂單金額動向指數分別為 52.4 及 51.5，顯
示科技需求仍具支撐；基本金屬與化學品則達 59.0 及 61.0，呈現部分材料領域接單改善的期待。若
AI 伺服器出貨 、 自研晶片開發及半導體資本支出持續推進 ， 相關台灣供應鏈的營收與獲利有望受惠。

另一方面，機械與光學器材指數分別為 46.3 及 49.5，提醒市場不能將整體訂單成長視為所有產業同
步復甦，仍須以後續實績檢驗。
投資策略方面，10 月以「掌握 AI 主線、尋找擴散機會、重視獲利驗證」作為配置方向。核心部
位可留意伺服器、半導體及 IC 設計等需求較明確的企業，並觀察 ASIC 相關業務能否由開發逐步轉
化為穩定出貨；其他機會則可從設備、銅箔與高階材料中，篩選確實取得訂單的公司。選股不只看產
業接單增幅，更應比較個別企業的營收成長、毛利率與獲利展望，避免在評價已高時追逐短期題材。
整體而言，台股仍有基本面支撐，後續行情能否擴大，關鍵在於 AI 需求能否持續轉化為更多企業的
實際獲利。

中國股市：AI 產業規模擴張難掩獲利困境，產業進入變現考驗
9 月中國 AI 產業的討論，從模型能力與使用量，逐漸轉向更根本的問題：在高階晶片取得受限、
業者競爭激烈的環境下，龐大的投入能否形成可持續的回報。9 月中美高層會談後，雙方宣布建立
AI 相關對話機制，但公開成果未顯示高階 AI 晶片出口限制已放寬。政策溝通有所進展，並未立即改
變企業取得先進算力的條件 。 與此同時 ， 低價及開放權重策略持續降低AI 使用門檻 ， 帶動市場擴張，
也使企業更難單靠模型服務取得理想利潤。對投資人而言，9 月最重要的訊號不是單一產品的表現，

而是中國 AI 已進入需要同時回答算力效率、商業變現與資本回收的階段。
從整體結構來看，中國具備電力、製造基礎及廣泛應用場景，有利於 AI 以較低成本進入企業營
運與日常服務；但部署成本較低，不代表產業獲利便能同步提高。當業者以低價爭取用戶，使用量可
以迅速累積，收入卻可能跟不上基礎設施、研發及營運支出。以特定平台的觀察為例，DeepSeek 曾
取得可觀的代幣使用量，但相應支出占比明顯較低；這不能代表中國 AI 產業的整體財務表現，卻凸
顯「有人使用」與「願意付費」之間仍有距離。未來產業能否改善獲利，取決於企業是否找到具持續
需求的應用、建立合理收費方式，並避免同質化服務陷入反覆降價。
展望 10 月 ， 中國AI 的成長空間仍在 ， 但市場衡量進展的標準應由使用規模 ， 逐步轉向投入產出。
若 AI 更深入企業工作流程，相關需求有望延續；然而，需求增加後，業者仍須證明服務品質、運算
效率與客戶付費能同步改善 。 晶片限制使算力使用效率更加重要 ， 價格競爭則使收入品質成為另一項
考驗。Vercel 資料顯示，8 月開放權重模型在其平台占代幣使用量 56%，支出占比卻為 14%，說明

高使用量未必對應高支出；10 月宜觀察企業是否開始披露更清楚的付費需求與成本改善，而非只比
較流量或模型排名。
投資策略方面，對中國 AI 宜維持關注成長、審慎評估獲利的態度，避免把產業滲透率提高，直
接等同於所有企業的投資價值提升。評估重點可放在三個整體問題：第一，持續增加的算力與研發投
入，能否換來足夠的收入；第二，低價競爭下，企業能否留住付費客戶並維持合理利潤；第三，AI
應用能否從試用走向長期採購。具備穩定需求、成本控制能力及清楚收費模式的企業，較有機會在產
業整合中建立優勢。整體而言，中國 AI 並非缺少使用場景，而是仍須證明大規模使用可以轉化為可
持續的獲利，這將是 10 月觀察產業與相關市場表現的核心。

科技類股：AI 應用加速落地，光通訊與 Agent 入口迎來成長機會
AI 產業的成長主軸正由模型訓練延伸至推論與實際應用 ， 讓軟體服務與硬體建設形成相互帶動的
循環。當 AI 不再只回答問題，而能協助搜尋資訊、安排活動及完成跨平台任務，使用頻率與運算需
求有望提高。Meta 於 9 月推出個人 AI 助理 Muse，透過應用程式及 WhatsApp 接觸既有用戶，積
極爭取 AI 服務入口。對產業而言，Agent 普及不僅意味新的服務形式，也可能擴大雲端運算與資料
傳輸需求，使應用端的創新成為基礎設施升級的推力。投資機會可從使用者入口延伸至相關供應鏈。

在硬體端，AI 推論與多步驟任務若持續增加，資料中心內部的資料交換將更頻繁，高速且節能的
連接能力因而更具價值 。 光通訊除支援資料中心之間的傳輸 ， 也逐步朝機櫃及晶片附近的互連場景發
展 ， 拓展供應商的產品空間 。Lumentum 指出 ，AI 運算對速度與頻寬的要求提升 ， 帶動光連結需求，
並已在營收展望中反映相關成長 。 高速光模組與新型互連方案也持續推進 。 雖然部分新技術仍須經過
客戶導入與量產驗證，但既有產品需求與後續技術升級有望接續，讓光通訊成為 AI 基礎設施中值得
持續關注的環節。
在應用端，Muse 代表 AI 從被動回覆走向主動協助執行任務的嘗試。若 Agent 能替使用者完成
搜尋、比較、預訂與付款等流程，便利性提升有望促進使用黏著度，並為支付、交易與服務串接平台
創造新的合作機會。跨平台操作可能由單一入口整合，促使各產業重新思考客戶服務。 「Muse 對各
產業的影響」圖可呈現商業模式轉變的可能方向。隨著更多服務接入 Agent 生態系，使用情境有望
持續擴展；後續若任務完成率、使用留存及付費轉換逐步改善，應用端便有機會形成更明確的收入來

源，並進一步支持運算與傳輸投資。
投資策略方面，建議以「應用入口與基礎設施並重」掌握 AI 從訓練走向推論的成長機會。硬體
方面，可優先關注已切入主要客戶供應鏈、具備高速光模組與新型互連產品布局，且訂單能見度較佳
的業者。應用方面，則可追蹤具備用戶基礎、服務整合能力與清楚收費模式的平台，觀察 Agent 能
否從新產品熱度轉化為持續使用與付費需求 。 配置上可先以業績較明確的基礎設施業者為核心 ， 再逐
步增加應用平台的布局，兼顧當前需求與後續成長空間。整體而言，AI 應用愈深入日常生活與企業
流程 ， 入口平台與光通訊供應鏈愈有機會相互受惠 ； 選股關鍵仍是找出能將需求成長轉化為實際業績
的企業。

拉美市場：10 月巴西總統大選將登場，政治不確定性影響市場表現
9 月拉美市場維持震盪整理 。 一方面 ， 拉美各國通膨數據普遍低於市場預期 ，有利市場降息預期；
另一方面，巴西總統大選選情持續拉鋸，政治不確定性升溫則讓投資人保持觀望。展望 10 月，隨巴
西大選投票日逼近，政治因素仍可能使拉美市場短線波動加劇。
受到美伊衝突帶動油價上漲影響，市場原本擔心燃料與運輸成本增加，會讓拉美物價再度大幅上
漲，進而迫使拉美央行升息。然而，高盛分析指出，拉美市場目前的通膨率仍受到控制，當地經濟承
受能源漲價的能力 ， 比市場原先擔心的情況更好 。 其中一個重要原因是 ， 新興市場政府透過燃油補貼 、

稅制安排與零售價格管制，讓消費者實際感受到的價格漲幅相對有限。此外，上半年全球農產品供給
較為充裕，使食品價格漲幅偏低，也抵銷了部分能源漲價帶來的影響。由於拉美市場實際通膨壓力小
於原先市場預期，央行進一步大幅升息的必要性也隨之降低，高盛因此認為，市場目前對多數拉美經
濟體未來一至兩年的升息預期過於悲觀，並預期拉美各國央行在 2027 年較可能重新轉向降息。
不過，拉美多國經濟出現放緩跡象，亦可能壓抑股市上漲空間。智利央行於 9 月會議維持政策利
率 4.50%不變 ， 並將2026 年經濟成長預測下修至 0.25%至 0.75%， 低於6 月預估的 1.0%至 1.75%。
智利央行指出，第二季內需成長放緩，勞動市場及信心指標同步轉弱，加上燃料價格上漲侵蝕企業與
家庭所得，均對經濟活動形成壓力。巴西經濟亦出現放緩跡象，巴西央行於 9 月 16 日一致決議調降
Selic 政策利率 25 個基點至 13.75%，並指出景氣循環產業動能轉弱，且整體與核心通膨均已回落至
目標區間上限以下，為進一步降息提供空間。不過，巴西央行並未承諾下次會議是否將繼續降息，而

是強調將視未來經濟與通膨數據逐次評估。
另一方面，巴西總統大選進入白熱化階段則是當前影響市場最主要的因子。市場民調顯示，相較
7 月時現任總統魯拉在民調中普遍領先競爭者波索納洛約 5 至 8 個百分點 ，在 9 月的民調雙方差距已
縮小至 2 個百分點以內，選情不確定性仍高。若選舉最終由魯拉勝選，其支持財政擴張的政策，將可
能使市場對於巴西財政赤字問題的擔憂加劇，進而使巴西股市面臨較大壓力；而若波索納洛勝選，市
場普遍預期其能提出改善投資人信心的財政政策 ， 為巴西央行創造更多降息空間 ， 有利後續巴西股市
上行。
整體而言，儘管市場對拉美 各國當前升息預期可能過於悲觀，但目前拉美多國經濟成長同步放
緩，加上巴西總統大選選情膠著，選後財政政策方向不確定性高，在考量大選及政策方向不確定性的
狀況下，短線拉美市場波動恐將加劇。

能源類股：全球石油庫存持續下降，但中東石油出口已出現回升
9 月西德州原油期貨價格受美伊衝突升溫影響，一度於月中重新站上每桶 100 美元，其後隨衝突
趨緩，油價漲幅逐步收斂，月底約回落至90 美元附近，能源類股表現亦大致跟隨油價走勢。展望10
月，近期中東石油供應恢復速度不如預期，加上庫存持續下降，預期油價下檔仍具支撐。然而，市場
數據顯示，高油價及供應受阻已同步抑制石油需求，且中東各國原油出口持續回升，後續供應緊張情
況有望逐步改善。因此，雖然中東地緣政治風險仍將對油價形成支撐，但隨美伊重啟外交接觸，油價
進一步大幅上漲的空間預期相對有限。

受到美伊談判僵持影響，國際能源署（IEA）與美國能源資訊署（EIA）在 9 月月報均將中東石油
供應恢復的預期時間延後。IEA 將中東石油供應全面恢復的預期時間延至 2027 年，並將 2026 年全
球石油供應減幅，由上月預估的每日減少 430 萬桶擴大至 570 萬桶；EIA 則將中東石油供給恢復至
衝突前水準的時間，由 2027 年初延至第 2 季。
此外 ，受到中東供應受阻與油價偏高影響 ，IEA 亦將 2026 年全球石油需求預測下修每日 94 萬桶 ，
預估全年需求將較去年減少每日 250 萬桶，EIA 則預估全年石油需求將減少約每日 170 萬桶，石油
輸出國組織（OPEC）也將今年需求由上月預估的每日增加 60 萬桶下修至 40 萬桶。
受此影響，全球石油庫存持續下降。IEA 估計，8 月全球可觀察石油庫存減少 9,500 萬桶，自 2
月底以來已累計下降 5.07 億桶，顯示市場仍持續動用庫存填補缺口。EIA 也預期全球石油庫存將持
續下降至年底，因此將2026 年下半年布蘭特原油均價預測，由上月約每桶82 美元上調至 90 美元；

此外 ，隨著 2027 年中東出口與產量逐步恢復 ，IEA 預估明年全年布蘭特原油均價將回落至 74 美元。
另一方面，中東原油出口則已經出現回升跡象。路透社 9 月 28 日引述 Kpler 初步資料指出，沙
烏地阿拉伯與阿聯酋 9 月原油出口均出現上升 ， 帶動中東主要產油國當月原油日均出口量回升至每日
1,280 萬桶，創下 2 月戰爭爆發以來最高水準。其中，沙烏地阿拉伯9 月原油日均出口預估將達每日
540 萬桶，較 8 月的 244.6 萬桶增加逾一倍。由於沙國東西向輸油管線先前因攻擊受損，沙烏地計
畫將部分原先經紅海延布港出口的原油 ， 轉往波斯灣沿岸港口裝運 ， 帶動拉斯坦努拉港9 月日均出貨
量預估由 8 月的每日 92.9 萬桶增至 360 萬桶。
整體而言，中東供應恢復時程延後，加上全球石油庫存持續下降，短期仍有利支撐油價。然而，
石油需求預估同步下修 ， 且沙烏地阿拉伯與阿聯酋原油出口均已逐步回升 ，後續石油供應緊張程度可
望有所緩解。整體而言，儘管油價仍受中東地緣風險支撐，但隨美國期中選舉接近，近期美伊亦重啟

外交接觸，市場對原油供應中斷的擔憂可望逐步降溫，油價進一步大幅上漲空間相對有限。

### Page 10

任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行及本行之任何董事或受僱人，毋須負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決策，應審慎衡量本
身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元大銀行所有，非經本行同意，
不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕不另行通知。
黃金類股：升息預期攀升壓抑金價表現；財富配置及央行買盤支撐中長期需求
近期黃金價格表現較為疲弱，主要受到市場升息預期持續攀升，帶動美債殖利率走高，增加持有
黃金的機會成本，對於利率較為敏感的黃金 ETF 需求因此受到壓抑。然而，高盛研究指出，聯準會
近期決議升息雖然延長了市場高利率環境時間，但並不會進一步墊高利率終點 ， 加上全球央行持續購
金，以及投資人從財富配置角度增加黃金配置，均有助於支撐中期黃金需求。
高盛報告指出，聯準會於 9 月升息一碼，增加持有黃金的機會成本，使對利率敏感的黃金ETF 需
求下滑 ， 高盛因此將2026 年底金價預估由每盎司 4,900 美元下修至 4,650 美元 。 然而 ， 高盛也預估，

聯準會將於 2027 年 9 月至 2028 年 3 月期間降息三碼，使政策利率最終回到 3.25%至 3.5%區間，
故儘管聯準會 9 月升息延長了高利率環境，但實際並未墊高利率終點，因此來自黃金 ETF 的賣壓僅
會延後金價上漲時程，但並未改變高盛對黃金的偏多看法。
另一方面，央行買盤仍是金價支撐核心。高盛9 月報告預估，全球央行近三個月經季節調整後的
購金速度已上升至每月 91 噸，遠高於 2022 年前每月 17 噸的平均水準。高盛指出，這股需求主要
來自分散外匯儲備的考量，在 2022 年俄羅斯央行資產遭美國凍結後，各國央行更加重視儲備資產遭
制裁或凍結的風險，相較於對利率敏感的 ETF 資金，央行基於儲備分散而進行的購金，不易因短期
利率變動而轉向，因此即使聯準會升息，央行買盤仍有利支撐金價表現。
除了央行買盤外，花旗也從全球財富配置的角度看好黃金。花旗指出，全球每年新增黃金礦產供
應規模相當有限，若全球家庭額外將 0.1%的財富配置於黃金，就相當於約 4,459 噸黃金購買量，也

等於 1.2 年的新增礦產供應量。換言之，即使全球投資人僅小幅提高黃金配置比重，也可能創造遠超
過新增供給的需求。由於黃金供給短期內難以快速增加，市場往往需要透過金價上漲，吸引既有持有
人釋出黃金以滿足新增需求，因此資產配置需求增加可能對金價產生顯著推升影響。
不過，花旗也指出，黃金新增供給有限的特性雖有助於放大需求對金價的推升效果，但同時也可
能提高金價出現較劇烈修正的風險。花旗估計，2022 年以來的金價上漲已使既有黃金持有人的帳面
財富增加約 13 兆美元，若持有人將其中約 1 兆美元轉回股票、債券或現金，所形成的黃金賣壓便可
能超過目前全部實體黃金需求，故當持有人集中獲利了結時，金價也可能出現較劇烈的修正。
整體而言，在市場升息預期持續攀升的情況下，短期黃金ETF 需求可能受到壓抑，金價仍可能面
臨震盪 。 然而 ， 全球央行基於外匯儲備分散需求持續購金 ， 加上投資人從財富配置角度提高黃金配置 ，
均有助於支撐中期黃金需求。

### Page 11

任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行及本行之任何董事或受僱人，毋須負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決策，應審慎衡量本
身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元大銀行所有，非經本行同意，
不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕不另行通知。
固定收益：聯準會升息穩定通膨預期，凸顯中天期美債與投資級債吸引力
美國 9 月聯準會貨幣政策會議以 12 比 0 全票通過 ， 將聯邦基金利率目標區間上調 25 個基點至
3.75%–4.00%。最新經濟預測同步將 2026 年核心 PCE 上修至 3.4%、GDP 成長率上修至 2.3%，且
全面上修 2026 至 2027 年政策利率預估至 4.1%，並暗示年底前仍有一次升息空間。9 月聯準會採取
預防性升息，主要是在避免通膨預期鬆動並維持政策公信力，確立高利率維持更久的政策基調。
美國政府於 9 月 30 日公布第二季國內生產毛額(GDP)終值，經季節調整後成長年率由 1.5%上修

至 2.2%，高於經濟學家預估的維持不變。第一季 GDP 季增年率也由 2.1%上修至 2.5%。其中，占
美國經濟活動逾 6 成的消費支出，第二季成長率由 3.4%上修至 3.8%，顯示美國消費動能強勁；元
大投顧指出，美國 8 月核心資本財訂單月增率加速至 1.6%，其中初級金屬、機械及電機設備訂單均
呈現增長，顯示 AI 相關投資及企業設備更新需求持續支撐資本財訂單，企業設備投資可望對經濟成
長形成穩健貢獻。在消費與企業動能表現強勁下，使市場重新評估美國經濟對通膨黏性的影響。
彭博 10 月 1 日的報告指出，在 Market Pulse 調查的 173 名受訪者，超過一半的人預測美國 30
年期公債殖利率在年底將達到 6%。儘管伊朗戰爭和高借貸成本持續影響 ， 美國經濟仍維持強勁成長 ，
這種韌性加劇了通膨和進一步升息的⾵險；人工智慧基礎設施建設競賽引發了企業發債的狂潮 ，推升
長天期債券發行量，全球企業今年以來已發行超過 4000 億美元的債券，用於技術投資，而其中大部
分債券發行於美國。另外，各國政府持續的預算赤字，美國聯邦政府債務規模首次突破 40 兆美元，

美國政府需要擴大發債量 ， 投資人要求更高的利率來購買債券等因素 ，皆為推升美國長天期殖利率上
行的原因。因此，長天期債券後續表現仍須持續觀察利率走勢、債券供需變化及通膨發展。
展望 10 月，由於美國通膨仍高於聯準會 2%的目標，2026 年底有再升息一碼的可能，使得短
天期利率下行空間有限。在政策利率可能維持高檔、長天期殖利率又受到供給面壓力推升的環境下，
當前的債券配置應以「息收防禦」與「存續期間控管」為核心。隨著美國公債利率上行，彭博美國投
資級債指數平均剩餘到期期限持續下降 ， 存續期間縮短有助降低利率變動對債券價格的影響 。美國債
市投資建議重點配置中天期美國公債與投資級公司債 。中天期美國公債與投資級公司債不僅能鎖定具
吸引力的收益率水準，也能兼顧存續期間風險控管，降低利率波動對投資組合淨值的衝擊，在當前環
境下有助於兼顧收益與風險控制。

9 月 PMI 数据点评

建筑业景气度回升至扩张区间   
9 月制造业生产指数走高， 需求整体仍位于扩张区间。9 月建筑业景气度年内
首次回升至扩张区间。 
◼ 制造业生产指数走高。9 月制造业 PMI 指数为 50.1%， 环比 （8 月， 下同）
上升 0.3 个百分点，制造业景气度回升至扩张区间。 
制造业需求整体仍位于扩张区间。着眼重要细分项， 9 月新订单指数
50.5%， 环比下滑0.1 个百分点；9 月新出口订单指数环比回落 0.1 个百分
点，当月值为 50.0%；9 月产成品库存指数 47.6%，环比下滑 0.8 个百分
点； 制造业需求整体仍位于扩张区间。值得一提的是，9 月非制造业中建
筑业 PMI 指数为 50.3%，年内首次回升至扩张区间，固定资产投资发力
或是 9 月国内需求的重要支撑。 
◼ 9 月非制造业 PMI 指数回升至扩张区间。9 月非制造业 PMI（商务活动）
指数为 50.2%，环比上月走高 1.2 个百分点。从需求端看，9 月新订单指
数为 46.5%， 环比走高2.4 百分点。 外需方面， 新出口订单指数为48.5%，
环比上月走高 1.5 个百分点。9 月非制造业从业人员指数为 45.9%，环比
上月走高 0.5 个百分点， 但仍处于较深的收缩区间。供应商配送时间指数
51.3%，环比回升 0.1 个百分点。 
◼ 9 月建筑业景气度年内首次回升至扩张区间。 建筑业PMI 指数 50.3%，环
比回升 3.4 个百分点，建筑业景气度年内首次回升至扩张区间。9 月新订
单指数 45.7%，环比走高 3.3 个百分点。从业人员指数 44.3%，环比改善
1.3 个百分点。此外，业务活动预期指数 55.4%，环比改善 3.6 个百分点，
9 月建筑业多个分项指标表现改善， 新订单、 从业人员指数及业务活动预
期均有回升，或能在一定程度上反映出 9 月固定资产投资进度加快。 
◼ 服务业景气度回升至扩张区间。9 月服务业 PMI 指数 50.2%，环比回升
0.9 个百分点，服务业景气度回升至扩张区间。新订单指数 46.7%，环比
回升 2.2 个百分点；销售价格指数环比回升 1.4 个百分点，至 50.6%；从
业人员指数环比回升 0.4 个百分点，为 46.2%。 
◼ 风险提示：海外大宗商品价格震荡；地缘关系的不确定性；政策效果不
及预期。

### Page 2

2026 年 10 月 8 日 9 月 PMI 数据点评 2

9 月制造业生产指数走高，需求整体仍位于扩张区间 
制造业生产指数走高。9 月制造业 PMI 指数为 50.1%，环比（8 月，下同）上升 0.3 个百分点，制造
业景气度回升至扩张区间。 
分产业看，9 月高技术制造业 PMI 为 52.5%，环比下滑 0.4 个百分点，但仍高于制造业总体水平，
且维持在较高景气度区间，仍是当前制造业景气度回升的重要驱动；原材料行业 PMI 为 48.0%，仍
处于收缩区间。值得一提的是，9 月消费品行业 PMI 为 50.7%，环比上升 1.7 个百分点， 行业景气度
回升至扩张区间，或受到中秋、国庆假期因素带动；此外，9 月装备制造业 PMI 为 51.0%，较 8 月
小幅下滑 0.4 个百分点，但仍然维持在扩张区间；我们认为，9 月高技术制造业、装备制造业及消费
品行业均受假期因素干扰，四季度扩内需政策发力或仍对相关产业景气度有所带动。 
制造业需求整体仍位于扩张区间 。着眼重要细分项，9 月新订单指数 50.5%，环比下滑 0.1 个百分点；
9 月新出口订单指数环比回落 0.1 个百分点，当月值为 50.0%；9 月产成品库存指数 47.6%，环比下
滑 0.8 个百分点；制造业需求整体仍位于扩张区间。值得一提的是，9 月非制造业中建筑业 PMI 指
数为 50.3%，年内首次回升至扩张区间，固定资产投资发力或是 9 月国内需求的重要支撑。 
9 月生产指数 51.7%，环比回升 1.3 个百分点；原材料库存指数 48.2%，环比回升 0.1 个百分点；从
业人员指数 48.4 %，环比下滑 0.3 个百分点； 供货商配送时间指数50.1%，与 8 月持平。 企业预期方
面，制造业生产经营活动预期指数 53.8%，持平于 8 月，制造业经营预期维持在较高景气度区间。
从生产相关的细分指标看，月内在手订单和采购指数环比分别变动-0.7 和+0.5 个百分点。 
9 月生产指数环比走高，制造业采购备货意愿较强，且加速消化在手订单，或在一定程度上受到假
期因素影响，中秋、国庆假期时间较长，企业赶工意愿或较强。

价格指数方面，9 月主要原材料购进价格指数 60.8%,环比明显走高 4.2 百分点， 在较高景气区间继续
增长；制造业出厂价格指数也在扩张区间继续走高，环比增长 3.6 个百分点，当月值为 54.0%。 
综合来看，首先，9 月主要原材料购进价格指数涨幅仍然大于出厂价格指数，上游成本端推动或仍
是月内价格指数回升的重要因素。其次，需求回升对 9 月出厂价格指数的表现或仍存在支撑；9 月
新订单指数环比微幅回落，但仍维持在扩张区间，且在一定程度上受到外需拖累；同期产成品库存
指数下滑 0.8 个百分点，需求韧性带动产成品库存消化，制造业“被动去库存”现象仍存，下游或
仍具备一定的议价能力。

### Page 3

2026 年 10 月 8 日 9 月 PMI 数据点评 3

重点行业盈利能力方面，9 月专用设备制造业短期、长期盈利能力均较领先，或得益于 9 月建筑业
景气度回升；同期汽车制造业短期、长期盈利能力也在各行业中较为领先，汽车产业链盈利能力延
续改善；此外，计算机通信电子设备及仪器仪表制造业 短、长期盈利能力在各行业中均处于中等偏
上位置。长期盈利方面，有色金属冶炼及压延加工业盈利领先于其他行业。

中    性：预计该公司股价在未来 6-12 个月内相对基准指数变动幅度在-10%-10%之间；

## Document 154: AI boom or doom_ what will superintelligence feel like and when will we get there_ (2026-10-12)

Source PDF: all report pdf/AI boom or doom_ what will superintelligence feel like and when will we get there_ (2026-10-12).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-10-12
Pages: 20
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

AI boom or doom: what will 
superintelligence feel like and  
when will we get there?

1 
Authors 
Adrian Cox 
Research Analyst

Azeem Azhar, Exponential View 
Expert Collaborator

October 12, 2026 
The recent panic about rogue AI agents collaborating in swarms to take over the 
internet and threaten human existence has, more than occasionally, lacked 
nuance. But it does reflect how artificial intelligence is progressing in creative 
and surprising ways, and at a pace that seems uncontrollable. 
For some in the industry, it is evidence that the technology is following a path of 
accelerating self-improvement that will lead to artificial general intelligence (AGI) 
and, perhaps, to utopia or catastrophe. 
Former Google CEO Eric Schmidt has sketched out a “San Francisco 
Consensus” based on a belief in scaling laws, with ever larger AI models 
becoming an input into their own improvement; superintelligence by the end of 
the decade; and transformative AI systems outperforming humans on many 
tasks. This loop would lead to exponential growth in scientific advancement, 
financial returns and human progress. And crucially, AGI, and then 
superintelligence, would merely be milestones in a process, far from its climax. 
If that were the case, superintelligence could make much of the cognitive work 
that dominates the world economy much cheaper and change the ways in which 
firms compete. But its economic effects will depend on how businesses and 
customers respond. 
In this paper, we analyse what the San Francisco Consensus means for investors 
and enterprises in the years to come and what could accelerate – or slow down – 
its timeline of AGI by 2030. 
1. What would superintelligence look and feel like? 
2. Can we get there theoretically? 
3. Can we get there practically? 
4. What happens if we don’t get there? The base case 
The question is urgent. 
And it is complicated by reports of AI models autonomously accessing the 
internet and hacking other companies; a public backlash over the energy-
intensive data centres springing up as part of a historic $1 trillion a year 
investment boom; and dire warnings of threats to life if superintelligent AI runs 
roughshod over its human creators to achieve its own goals.

### Page 2

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 2

1. What would superintelligence look and feel like? 
1.1 What are AGI and superintelligence? 
Artificial general intelligence (AGI) is a simple milestone scribbled in pencil: an AI 
system that has surpassed human capabilities. Superintelligence is the 
continuum beyond that point. It is not merely systems that exceed the best 
human performance across one cognitive task or another, but system s that 
exceed human potential across every cognitive task. 
The San Francisco Consensus takes this further: the intelligence must be able to 
produce more intelligence. The key to getting there is so -called recursive self -
improvement, where the AI is able to autonomously design and improve itself in 
a way that compounds exponentially. A first step has maybe already been taken, 
with OpenAI saying recently that AI is now writing approximately 80 percent of 
the company’s code. 
There is significant disagreement on how we will be able to identify AGI or 
superintelligence when we see it, let alone what comes afterwards.  
OpenAI’s Charter defines AGI in economic terms, as “highly autonomous systems 
that outperform humans at the most economically valuable work”. Google 
researchers proposed a five -step ladder based on performance and generality, 
with level 5 “superhuman” for a system that outperforms 100% of humans. And 
philosopher Nick Bostrom, who got the ball rolling in 2014 with his influential 
book “Superintelligenc e”, defined it as “any intellect that greatly exceeds the 
cognitive performance of humans in virtually all domains of interest”. 
It’s also a moving target.  
In August, OpenAI CEO Sam Altman said the company would have an internal 
system that he would call AGI by later this year but subsequently told a podcast 
that “at best it’s a very poorly defined term. I was going to say it’s like an irrelevant 
marketing term”. Yet his No. 2 Greg Brockman described the new Astra model at 
the start of September as being potentially the moment that “AGI was created”. 
For this report, we have chosen to focus on a system that outperforms essentially 
all humans across essentially all cognitive tasks, including the development of AI 
itself.  
Under our scenario, by 2030 the best performing AI models would have the 
qualities of “superintelligence”, that is better than human experts in all domains. 
These would be frontier models: and using them would feel exactly like bringing 
the best human experts in a room to solve whatever business problem you faced.

And the equivalents of today’s frontier models would be available, quite literally, 
everywhere, compressed enough to run on every phone, tablet or laptop 
computer.  
In early 2026, we already see early examples of what highly capable AI can do. 
They give a flavour for a world with superintelligence even if the reality would be 
a leap beyond, both quantitatively and qualitatively. At that point, 
superintelligence would mark a regime break, not a faster version of what we 
already have. Rather than blindly doing what humans specify, but just a bit better, 
it would specify the task itself.

### Page 3

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 3

Figure 1: What changes when cognitive work gets abundantly cheaper?

Source: Deutsche Bank Research

Meanwhile cost may be less and less of a constraint as ever greater machine 
intelligence becomes cheaply available. Of measured technologies, no 
technology has got cheaper faster than AI has, almost halving in price every 
quarter for the past three years on a like-for-like basis, or 13 times per year, Epoch 
data show. 
If these systems can be supplied widely, firms will be able to buy more of the 
complex, expensive and rare analysis, design and engineering kinds of services 
than they do today. Activities that were previously uneconomic could become 
viable, and in a competitive market, savings could pass to customers. The main 
driver will then be whether demand grows fast enough to preserve margins. 
However, superior capability, doesn’t, on its own, automatically lead to the 
adoption of cheap, abundant intelligence. Tasks that do not yet yield to super-

### Page 4

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 4

intelligence, perhaps because they require real world-coordination, will therefore 
end up commanding significant premia. 
At that point, cognitive labour stops being scarce and the bottleneck becomes 
the physical and practical assets that cannot be conjured by up more compute, 
such as electricity, land, steel and permits. 
1.2 Capability and diffusion 
If AI becomes capable of improving itself, the technology may compound faster 
and faster. The supply of machine intelligence could grow faster than business 
and physical infrastructure can accommodate.  
This balance between capability and diffusion creates different scenarios.

Figure 2: The balance between capabilities and diffusion

Source: Deutsche Bank Research

Lower capability, constrained diffusion: where we are now 
Progress is steady and sector-by-sector. This is how technology usually diffuses, 
according to researchers for the New York Fed back in 2003, who tracked 20 
major technologies across leading industrial economies from 1788 and found “a 
pattern of trickle-down diffusion that is remarkably robust across technologies”. 
Lower capability, high diffusion: the cloud transition 
This is faster because the complementary investment is small, with no retooling 
and no skills at scale required. Amazon Web Services launched the modern cloud 
in 2006, offering scalable computing and storage infrastructure. Adoption grew 
and then accelerated over the pandemic. Almost all enterprises now use it  in 
some form.

### Page 5

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 5

Higher capability, lower diffusion: the overhang 
This is theoretically possible and arguably where we are today in narrow domains. 
The technology would be applied in some sectors of the economy, while other 
sectors would move at a slower speed.  Superintelligent or not, it risks being 
bogged down by physical constraints, bureaucracy and human inertia. 
Higher capability, higher diffusion: superintelligence 
In this scenario, capability becomes so high that it changes the dynamics of 
diffusion itself. AI generates not just better AI (so -called recursive self -
improvement, or RSI) but also speeds up the integration process. 
Rapid AI diffusion could work two ways. 
In the first, AI could be enlisted to do integration work itself: mapping processes, 
rebuilding data work, retraining staff, writing the control and audit layer, and so 
on. The problem with this is that absorbing and recreating undocumented 
knowledge is impossible by definition, at least for a task-based superintelligence.  
The second way that higher capability AI could be rapidly diffused is much more 
competitive – and disruptive. Rather than incumbents slowly converting AI, 
perhaps using it for some of the hard work of integration, they could be instead 
displaced by new firms that have been built AI-native from day one. New entrants 
will never have to incur integration costs because there is nothing to integrate. 
Incumbents will need to change – fast – or die.  
Change could also be forced by earlier adopters in the supply chain, as happened 
with one company after another adopting electronic data interchange (EDI) to 
link with its suppliers or customers, or by a migration of capital towards early 
adopters, reprici ng the cost of capital for slow -coaches and prodding them to 
move faster. 
1.3 The forcing mechanism: change or die 
A company that uses superintelligence may outperform one that doesn’t. 
Firms using superintelligence will likely have substantial cost and revenue -
harvesting advantages. Every firm will therefore have to adopt superintelligence 
or retrench to a smaller set of offerings. And any business line of lasting value 
abandoned by a no n-adopter will be taken over by an adopter. Customer inertia 
will provide a safe harbour for a limited time only.  
The analogy here is electricity. There came a point in the deployment of 
electricity where firms in every sector, from entertainment to finance, from 
healthcare to transport, had to adopt the technology to remain relevant.  
Under this scenario, wait -and-see will not be an option. There will be no stable 
“non-adoption” alternative. Sufficiently high capabilities will force a tipping point 
of rapid diffusion and competitive repricing. Firms using yesterday’s production 
model will either have to change, choose a different market or die.  
Of course, caveats apply.  
Customers may not change behaviour immediately. They may even prefer 
companies which limit their use of AI, in the same way that some customers 
choose to avoid factory-produced food.

### Page 6

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 6

The paradox that Nobel Prize-winning economist Robert Solow observed in 1987, 
that “you can see the computer age everywhere but in the productivity statistics”, 
is likely to still apply for a while – even with superintelligence. 
The reason is that the productivity J -curve identified by Stanford University AI 
economy guru Erik Brynjolfsson et al is unavoidable. In that scenario, the drag of 
early intangible investments to integrate general purpose technologies means 
that productivity initially appears to slow before rapidly accelerating.

Figure 3: Productivity J-curve: slow then fast

Source: Brynjolfsson E, Rock D, Syverson C, “The Productivity J-Curve” (2018, revised 2020), Deutsche Bank Research

Furthermore, “change or die” would require abundant capital and low regulatory 
entry points for nimble new entrants. But in practice, highly regulated areas such 
as banking, utilities, pharmaceuticals and defence are tough nuts to crack. 
Existing contracts and licences, high switching costs and decades of depreciated 
capital investment and infrastructure would shield incumbents at least for a 
healthy transition period. 
For an extreme example from the real world of better, cheaper technology failing 
to force change, take Pakistan’s 3.6 -gigawatt imported coal power plant fleet 
installed in the mid -2010s. In that case, it came with expensive take -or-pay 
liabilities which wi ll continue to run for decades, even though changes like 
cheaper rooftop solar have cut utilisation to below 20 percent.  
So: superintelligence will not force adoption everywhere. It will hit hardest in 
contestable sectors, say, high -volume customer services, routine software 
engineering and freelance creative work, and widen the gap between them and 
protected ones. Forced adoption will be about relative value, not an apocalypse.

### Page 7

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 7

1.4 What survives and what it means for portfolios 
Because AI sits on an aggressive learning curve, its price on an equivalent quality 
basis is plummeting. In a world of recursive super intelligence this is likely to 
continue. Its price will fall to the cost of compute.  
What will retain its value will be whatever the model cannot replicate: 
1. Proprietary data 
2. The right to operate , particularly in heavily regulated sectors such as 
healthcare and finance. 
3. Sticky customer relationships,  such as those where mission -critical 
workflows and databases are deeply entwined. 
4. Excludable physical assets, such as machinery and land. 
5. In addition, humans have proven to have unlimited wants.  Each shift in 
technological capabilities or cultural norms has delivered new needs to 
be met. Some will be needs we can articulate today, such as originality or 
empathy, but new ones are likely to arise.  
If this forcing mechanism holds, firms will separate into two “bimodal” groups: 
adopters and casualties, with very little in between. 
This dispersion of performance may create challenges for investors. The four 
years since the launch of ChatGPT in November 2022 have seen the stocks of 
semiconductor and semiconductor equipment makers surge almost seven times 
while those of software makers  have only risen by 80 percent. Conventional  
diversification and volatility-based allocation may become ineffective strategies.

Figure 4: Total return for S&P 500 semiconductor vs software stocks since 
launch of ChatGPT on Nov 30, 2022

Source: Bloomberg Finance LP, Deutsche Bank Research

### Page 8

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 8

Diversification normally works because idiosyncratic risks are unrelated and 
average each other out. Here they would not: with one variable that overrides 
everything else, spreading capital across more names would just spread exposure 
rather than diluting it. 
Investors will need to consider adapting their approach in three ways: 
1. Classify investments as adopters or casualties rather than position off 
averages.  
• Under a normal distribution, errors are symmetric and partially 
cancel each other out. But under bimodality they do not: to put a firm 
in one category or another means getting it completely right or 
wrong. Position size should track confidence in the classification. 
2. Avoid reading calm price action as low risk.  
• Trailing volatility measures dispersion that has already been realised. 
Bimodal separation is a jump, where the two modes look identical 
right until the moment when they don’t. A classic example is Chegg, 
an educational software maker which traded like a normal small-cap 
stock until one day in May 2023 when it halved on a single day after 
management linked slowing growth in new customers to ChatGPT. 
3. Averaging down, ie  scooping up shares whose price has fallen, may no 
longer be prudent. 
2. Can we get there theoretically? 
2.1 Scaling laws 
Scaling laws have provided the blueprint for improving AI over the past decade. 
It was a relationship between the amount of data, the amount of compute and 
the complexity of an AI model somewhat predictably informed how powerful a 
model would become. Google DeepMind’s Chinchilla model in 2022 showed that 
you could compensate for having a smaller model by using substantially more 
data. 
Scaling is older than the chatbot. In 1950 Claude Shannon built Theseus, a 
mechanical mouse that learned its way through a maze by trial and error. Epoch 
AI reckons it took about 40 operations of compute, known as FLOPs, to train. Grok 
4, trained in 2025, took roughly ten trillion trillion times as much. Across those 25 
orders of magnitude, more compute has brought more capability.

• A falling price may be information that a business model is obsolete 
rather than an opportunity.

### Page 9

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 9

Figure 5: Computation used to train notable AI systems (petaFLOP): log scale

Source: Epoch AI (citing estimates from AI literature), Our World in Data, Deutsche Bank Research

It is the robustness of this relationship that has made larger investments in 
compute capex a sine qua non for AI companies. 
However, this is not a law of physics and nothing guarantees it will continue . 
Indeed, in the ChatGPT era, five different approaches to scaling have been 
applied. Older strategies yield worse results, and new techniques, such as post -
training refinements, have been developed. The consensus amongst American 
and Chinese AI labs is that these will hold.  
While this may be a consensus amongst the commercial labs, there is scientific 
disagreement that apply ing scaling laws to today’s large -language models can 
achieve superintelligence. 
Yann LeCun, a Turing Award winner and formerly Meta's chief AI scientist, is blunt: 
"LLMs are not a path to superintelligence or even human-level intelligence”. Even 
inside the labs, the recipe is contested. Demis Hassabis of Google DeepMind has 
said he expects "one or two more big breakthroughs" to be needed "in addition to 
scaling". Ilya Sutskever, a co -founder and former chief scientist of OpenAI, has 
said the field is going "back to the age of research again, just with big computers". 
This is a scientific argument we are not in a position to evaluate , but for the 
purposes of our scenario, we will side with the labs’ position. 
For the moment, there is strong evidence that capabilities have in fact 
accelerated over the past three years, as reasoning models have outpaced non -
reasoning models by two or three times, according to Epoch data.

### Page 10

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 10

2.2 Recursive self-improvement: AI improving AI 
A cocktail-party definition of recursive self-improvement (RSI) is AI that improves 
itself. The reality is that it can be used in two ways: 
1. Self-improving agents that get better at using themselves: showing 
smarter use of prompts, tools, memory and workflows. The model -
building process is untouched. 
2. True RSI improves the process that builds the models: designing 
experiments, curating data, optimising algorithms and designing chips in 
a compounding loop. 
The former is like a factory worker who gets better at the job by learning the quirks 
of the machinery, getting organised and making fewer mistakes . The latter is like 
the worker redesigning the machine tools and then using those to build the next 
generation of machines. 
True RSI is what OpenAI’s chief scientist Jacob Pachoki referred to last month in 
a blog post about  “An Alien Mind ”. “Automated AI research is a more dramatic 
form of scaling intelligence with compute,” he wrote. “And of course as a part of 
it, AI will improve the computational substrate itself,” he added. Pachoki 
represents a broadly held consensus view amongst America n and Chinese labs 
that RSI is both possible and necessary for AI development. 
• OpenAI (June 2026): “AI doing AI research will become the determining 
factor of the pace of progress within the next few years.” 
• Google DeepMind’s Demis Hassabis (May 2026): “All the leading labs are 
quite focused on [RSI]…” Humanity is standing in the “foothills of the 
singularity”. 
• Alibaba CEO Eddie Wu (Sept 2026): RSI is “a concrete path forward,” with 
models autonomously designing experiments and driving a cycle of self-
evolution. 
For now, self -improving agents are the AI du jour. Noisy claims about the vast 
majority of coding being done by agents do not always stand up in the light of day 
from companies still hiring human coders at breakneck pace, albeit no longer to 
do the most mundane parts of the job.  
A better metric may be METR’s task -completion time horizon. This is measure of 
the complexity of software tasks that an AI agent can complete on its own, using 
the baseline of how long it would take a human expert to finish the task, at a 50 
percent success rate. So far the most advanced models have reached about 16 
hours, up from OpenAI’s GPT-4’s four minutes in March 2023.

### Page 11

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 11

Figure 6: Task horizon of software tasks that leading frontier models can complete 50% of the time (log scale)

Source: METR, Deutsche Bank Research

But software engineers can hold off on updating their resumes a little longer: 
there is a long road between 50 percent accuracy and the 99 percent or 100 
percent needed for serious enterprise use at scale, with the horizon potentially 
getting further away the nearer you get. Also METR notes that measurements 
above 16 hours are unreliable with the current task suite. As is the case across AI, 
the instrument is running out before the question is settled.  
More broadly, there are significant limitations to RSI:  
1. Research is clock-bound.  
• A frontier training run can take weeks on thousands of specialised chips, 
and researchers often cannot design the next experiment until the 
previous one has been completed.  
• That means that the speed of the loop is set by experiments completed 
per hour or day, not by ideas generated.  
• Compute is a hard ceiling that needs to be bought, buil t and connected 
to a grid. 
2. The real world defies verifiability.  
• The loop compounds only when the system can check its own output 
cheaply and automatically. 
• It is no coincidence that AI loves maths and coding. It is much harder to 
verify outputs over a long time horizon in the real world: does this drug 
work or does this strategy succeed?  
• RSI compounds fastest where the economy cares least and slows where 
the returns are largest.

### Page 12

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 12

3. Sequences are consequential.  
• Amdahl’s law finds that speed -up is capped when processes run in a 
sequence.  
• If you automate 90 percent, the residual 10 percent still limits you to a 
ten-times faster process. Nine women cannot produce a baby in one 
month.  
• The Usain Bolt of AI still has to run alongside the comparative tortoises 
of the training run itself, chip fabrication and real -world validation. Ten 
times faster is still not a regime break. 
This is borne out by METR’s series. Doubling every seven months is fast 
exponential growth but a take -off would show the doubling time itself – the 
second derivative – shrinking. Horizons are not (yet) exploding. 
Philosopher Toby Ord shows that recursive self -improvement can only reach a 
singularity if each cycle of improvement takes less time than the last, shrinking 
fast towards zero. He argues that real feedback loops have a floor: chips take time 
to make and models take time to train. A I progress could still outrun an 
exponential, but it would not go vertical. 
2.3 Scaling plus RSI: a match made in the race to AGI? 
No scaling and RSI means no short timeline for AGI. Without scaling, more 
compute buys nothing and capital is wasted. Without RSI, AI capability will 
continue to improve at the pace of flesh -and-blood human researchers, 
augmented by AI tools but not replac ed. Only with both will it be possible to 
produce a system that improves faster than the humans building it. Without one 
or the other, no quantity of chips, energy or capital will fix it. 
3. Can we get there practically? 
3.1 Infrastructure, chips and energy 
There is no consensus on the amount of training required to reach AGI. Estimates 
suggest that training 2026's top models required between 10^26 and 10^27 
FLOPs. If we hypothesise that an "AGI -like model" would require three orders of 
magnitude more training, this would require 10^30 FLOPs.   
Consider today’s US AI infrastructure, which runs to approximately 10^22 FLOPs. 
If all of this was conceivably used for a 10^30 FLOP training run, it would take 
nearly four years to complete.  
The physical infrastructure to practically support such a training run would 
require enormous power, perhaps around 6GW, about 50% more than the four 
Vogtle nuclear power plants recently activated in the US. The cost of the 
compute infrastructure would run to between $300bn and $500bn.  
Today’s advanced chips would simply not suffice. Back-of-the-envelope maths 
suggests that even using Nvidia’s most advanced Vera Rubin chips, training such 
a model in four months would require 7.8m chips and 26GW of facility power. 
That would cost $900bn today and a rental equivalent cost of $90bn.  
Of course, frontier compute is growing rapidly, and price -performance is 
declining. If current trends hold, a 2030 -era cluster capable of such a run would

### Page 13

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 13

need perhaps $200bn in computer and power hardware for a training run costing 
about $20bn.

Figure 7: Technology cost index over time (years since start of price decline)

Source: Epoch AI, Deutsche Bank Research

The hyperscalers are leading the charge towards AGI. Alphabet, Amazon, Meta, 
Microsoft and Oracle are set to invest almost $800bn in capital expenditure this 
year, mostly for ever more powerful data centres, and over $1 trillion a year 
through 2030 and beyond.

Figure 8: Hyperscaler capital expenditure (including estimates)

Source: Bloomberg Finance LP, Deutsche Bank Research

### Page 14

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 14

By 2030, assuming no market retrenchment, this means additional $4 trillion to 
$6 trillion on AI compute capability invested. This infrastructure could 
accommodate a training run of 10^30 FLOPs with some comfort.  
But even if AI labs crack the secret sauce of RSI, and their finance chiefs are willing 
to invest in such large training runs, and hyperscalers are prepared to continue to 
build the infrastructure, the infrastructure needs to be built.  
Grid connection queues, turbine lead times and transmission build are measured 
in years, not the months it takes to assemble and air-freight the latest Nvidia chip. 
The lead time for substation transformers is currently as long as three years. 
Politics in the US has shifted towards slowing the pace of the AI build out and this 
force will run against the demands for larger data centres that would be essential 
for RSI.  
3.2 Capital 
Financing for AI has been abundant in the historic buildout of the past three years. 
However, it is still largely conditional not on revenue but on belief in a future 
payoff. 
RSI is a “key part of the investment thesis” in the “biggest scientific bet civilisation 
has ever made,” Google DeepMind’s chief strategy officer Jasjeet Sekhon said at 
a conference in August, The Information reported. “We have this danger we could 
hit an AI air pocket such that the expenditures happen but the revenues don’t 
show up.” 
An inflection point is approaching where the nature of investment changes from 
free cash flow and equity to debt, with all that entails in terms of exposure to 
rising rates and creditor appetite. The hyperscalers are reaching a point where 
capex is no long er covered by operating cashflow and where net debt exceeds 
earnings before interest, depreciation and amortisation (EBITDA). 
Alphabet, for example, posted its first quarterly free  cash outflow in nearly two 
decades in its most recent quarter, even as it lifted its full-year capex guidance to 
$205bn. It said it would continue to rent third -party capacity and reported a 
backlog of $514bn in contracted, not-yet-recognised data centre revenue. 
3.3 Data 
Data has been the new oil for some time now. But unfortunately for AI 
development it seems to be running out even faster. 
The effective stock of quality - and repetition -adjusted public human text was 
about 300 trillion tokens, with a vast 90 percent confidence interval of 100 trillion 
to 1,000 trillion, Epoch estimated in 2024. A token is roughly equivalent to three 
quarters of a word. By that count it was likely to be fully used between 2026 and 
2032.

### Page 15

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 15

Figure 9: Projections of stock of public text and data usage

Source: Epoch AI, Deutsche Bank Research

However, since then, rather than data dropping off a cliff, there has been a rise in 
synthetic data generation, transfer learning from data-rich domains such as video 
and telemetry, and data efficiency. While the data shortage has not been 
resolved, the immediate crisis has been averted. 
AI now makes much of its own training data. When DeepMind's AlphaGo became 
superhuman at playing the mind-bendingly complex board game Go, it did so not 
by playing human experts, but by playing millions and millions of games against 
itself. 
For many domains relating to coding and mathematical proofs where automatic 
checks are possible, such synthetic experience can move things forward.  That is 
the case with AI research, where the data may be the results of experiments, all 
of which are bought by compute. About 90% of OpenAI's 2024 research compute 
went on such experiments. 
And increasingly, people write the tests rather than the answers. By the summer 
of 2026, selling training data for practi ce tasks was a nearly $9bn revenue 
business. Firms such as Mercor hire experts to generate long tasks against which 
the AI can be trained. 
The challenge here is that trust in the checker becomes a scarce input.

### Page 16

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 16

As AI makes production cheaper, it also makes establishing that production is 
accurate or reliable more expensive.  Domains which can be checked 
mechanically, such as mathematics, will be opened up more quickly. But those 
which rely on expert verification, such as medicine, engineering or law, may 
progress more slowly. 
The likely implications are twofold: a slower rate of diffusion into economic 
domains that are less verifiable and a larger share of production costs devoted to 
assurance. 
Another issue is that s ystems that have to  grade themselves learn to game the 
process. T his was partially what happened when an OpenAI model escap ed a 
sandbox and hacked into a third-party website. Trust replaced data as a wall. 
There are also question marks over the quality of data that is recycled repeatedly 
through the system. Although humans continue to produce original content, half 
of internet articles are now primarily generated by AI, Graphite data show.

Figure 10: Human vs primarily AI-generated articles on the internet

Source: Graphite (citing 55.4k articles from Common Crawl examined by AI detectors), Deutsche Bank Research

3.4 An incomplete case 
This analysis shows that the San Francisco Consensus, which emphasises RSI and 
a short timeline to AGI, is neither predestined nor impossible.  
Rather it depends on whether the science is right, on whether scaling is all you 
need, and on whether the infrastructure and capital required for that scaling is 
deployed and accessible to the leading labs.  
There is, in other words, a possible rather than impossible, path to AGI, subject to 
those conditions.  
None of this proves that superintelligence will ever arrive. A buildup at this scale 
has few peacetime precedents and vastly exceeds the likes of the Manhattan 
Project and Apollo programme.

### Page 17

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 17

It is uncertain whether there will be enough demand to pay upfront for the 
investment in achieving RSI that would justify that investment. And whether the 
friction of verification, and inevitable declining marginal returns, act as a drag on 
what otherwise might be a runaway process. 
4. What happens if we don’t get there? The base case 
4.1 Separating AI progress from superintelligence 
So what if that thesis is wrong? Does the AI trade depend on a journey to AGI?  
AI can remain economically important even before RSI coupled with scaling laws 
lead to super intelligence . Companies do not need to wait for nebulous 
superintelligence to generate identifiable returns from AI in the medium term as 
they fold it into their systems, streamline workflows and roll out new business 
models. 
Investors already have exposure to RSI as a capital allocation because the leading 
US labs say RSI is a key part of their product strategy. And it is these labs whose 
products are catalysing and amplifying the infrastructure boom. 
Even r apid competitive diffusion does not mean every part of the physical 
economy changes instantly. 
The smartest superintelligence might identify the optimal buyout structure 
before breakfast, but it cannot force a financially constrained buyer to use it or 
persuade the seller to accept less than they have been expecting. Those 
challenges will be even more intractable where the parties are more complex, say, 
fiscally constrained sovereigns or foreign equity holders. 
4.2 Why the base case is attractive 
Superintelligence would be a chaotic accelerant to human society, and a 
potential regime break. But it is not the only scenario in which today’s investment 
in AI infrastructure makes economic sense. 
Adoption of AI tools by businesses is progressing approximately three times faster 
than the mobile, internet and cloud computing waves. From sector revenues 
below $1bn in 2022, the AI economy, excluding China, will likely exceed $200bn 
in revenues in 2026.  
Early winners are the cloud businesses. Google Cloud revenue jumped 82 percent 
from a year earlier in the most recent quarter as companies turned AI pilots into 
workflows. Amazon Web Services sales were up 37 percent and Microsoft’s AI 
business more than doubled. 
There is also slow, but increasing, evidence of a productivity uplift. The J-curve 
cuts both ways: front -loading investments is taking one step back to take two 
steps forward. The so -called complementary intangible investment that is 
(mis)measured as cost today to the detriment of the productivity statistics will 
flatter them in the years to come as it pays off. The base case does not require a 
regime break to show up as a productivity surprise, just for the accounting to 
catch up. 
The economic gains from AI that is less powerful than the AGI mooted by the labs 
would be substantial. And in this base case, they may also be attractive.

### Page 18

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 18

Hyperscaler capex is driving earnings at beneficiaries ranging from 
semiconductor makers to construction companies and accounting for between 
one-third and one -half of current US growth. The wealth effect of rising stock 
prices, with  the ratio of  US equity wealth to income rising from around 1.8 to 
almost 2.5 over the past three years, is supporting consumer spending. 
AI FOMO (fear of missing out) is buoying enterprise spending on cloud services 
and the groundwork for AI initiatives. Those are beginning to bear fruit in terms of 
personal productivity, cost savings and more targeted products and services. 
4.3 Investor watchpoints 
The arrival of ASI would be a disruptive economic shock. Investors need to be alert 
for the early signals. 
Rather than relying on a single probability forecast, investors should watch five 
indicators particularly closely for signs that AGI is nearing.  
1. AI speeds up AI 
• Track METR’s estimate of how much AI speeds up AI. Currently the 
capabilities of models are doubling roughly every three months. In a 
world of RSI, the pace of this speed-up will itself likely accelerate. 
2. AI can complete whole jobs 
• Track via the Remote Labor Index, a multi-sector benchmark. Currently it 
finds that the highest performing agent still only manages to automate 
20 percent of the real-world, economically valuable freelance projects it 
attempts. 
3. Compute remains scarce 
• On a path to superintelligence, demand for compute will continue to 
outrun supply. Track GPU one-year rental prices, up by about a third over 
the past year. 
4. Firms bifurcate 
• Leaders and laggards in the same industry diverge while averages stay 
the same. Look at revenue per employee in top - and bottom -quartile 
firms 
5. Junior hiring comes under significant pressure 
• … And then the pressure rises up the ranks.

### Page 3

3
Summary
Executive
Global wealth: a record on paper, less so in real terms
Global financial assets expanded to a record EUR268.4trn in 2025, expanding +8.6% from
the previous year, despite a challenging geopolitical and economic backdrop. Global
wealth creation ran on autopilot, driven by markets, which accounted for 4 out of 5 euros
of additional wealth, while fresh savings fell by -5.4% to EUR4.1trn. Yet the record looks
less impressive after inflation: nominal financial assets have increased by almost 50%

since 2019, but real growth amounts to just 23%, leaving purchasing power only 5% above
its 2021 level.
Portfolios set the tone
Portfolio composition increasingly determines who captures the gains from wealth
creation. In 2025, securities grew by +12.4%, more than twice as fast as deposits (+5.7%)
or insurance and pensions (+5.0%), pushing their share of global financial assets to a
record 46.9%. North America’s securities-heavy portfolios, with an allocation of 60.7%,

helped the region generate 51.4% of the global increase. Its real financial assets are now
21% above 2019 levels. Western Europe, by contrast, has barely recovered its purchasing
power, with a real increase of just 0.5%. Over the past decade, valuation gains accounted
for 71% of North American financial-asset growth, against 36% in Western Europe. As a
result US financial assets grew 3.1pp faster than Europe’s, despite Europeans’ greater
savings effort relative to existing assets, indicating that saving more does not necessarily

mean accumulating more wealth.
Asset growth surpasses debt
Assets raced ahead of debt, strengthening household balance sheets. Global household
liabilities increased by +3.4% to EUR56.9trn, considerably slower than financial assets.
The household-debt ratio fell to 60.9% of GDP , almost 13pps below its 2009 peak,
reflecting both restrained borrowing and growth in nominal economic activity. Net
financial assets consequently rose by +10.1% to EUR211.5trn. Debt can also impact the

wealth rankings: while Swiss households remain the wealthiest in the world in terms of
gross financial assets per capita, the US is ahead of Switzerland in net financial assets per
capita, as Swiss household debt per person is more than twice the American level.
Catch-up pace slowing
Globalization has changed the geography of wealth, but poorer economies are losing
catch-up speed. The ratio of average net financial assets per capita in advanced
economies to emerging markets fell from 62 in 2005 to 21 in 2015, but only to 17 by 2025.

Emerging markets nevertheless now account for 61.9% of the global middle-wealth class
and 32.1% of the high-wealth class, compared with less than 2% of the latter in 2005. So,
while wealth has become far more geographically diverse, the momentum of catch-up
itself has faded. Trade fragmentation, weaker cross-border investment and reorganized
supply chains are making convergence harder. Digital services and the green transition
could open new opportunities, but seizing them will depend on access to capital,

Chiara Rocca
Research Assistant
chiara.rocca@allianz.com

### Page 4

Allianz Research
4
Stubborn inequality
Global wealth concentration has eased, but national wealth inequality has barely moved
in two decades. The richest 10% still own 85.4% of global net financial assets. This is down
from 91.5% in 2005, but at the current pace of improvement it would take almost another
eight decades for global concentration to approach the levels observed within individual
countries. Meanwhile, the bottom half of the world‘s population covered by this report,

almost 3bn people, collectively holds virtually no net financial assets. Within borders,
distribution has proved almost immovable: the share held by the top-decile averaged
60.9% in 2025, against 60.4% in 2005, and the average-to-median ratio barely moved
from 3.11 to 3.12. Still, ownership patterns are not fate. Some countries demonstrate that
improvement is possible: the Netherlands reduced its top-decile share by 2.4pps to 56.1%,
while Ireland’s average-to-median ratio fell from 4.87 to 3.42. These exceptions have not,

however, translated into a broader reduction in national wealth inequality.
AI growing the gap
AI could put an end to the calm on the distributional front by shifting more value creation
from labor towards capital, in turn widening the divide between earners and owners.
Allianz Research estimates that AI could affect almost 1 in 4 jobs across major economies
over the next three years, mainly through reorganization. Displacement could temporarily
outpace job creation as firms deploy technology faster than workers can retrain. If

productivity gains flow mainly into profits rather than wages, asset owners could capture
disproportionate benefits. Policy must therefore support worker adjustment, avoid
tax incentives that favor replacing labor and broaden participation in capital returns.
Otherwise, AI could create substantial wealth while concentrating its rewards and
spreading disruption, putting social cohesion under pressure.
AI promise and risk
The outlook for global wealth remains solid, but AI now carries both the upside and

the risk that will shape it. Global financial assets are expected to grow by around +9%
in 2026, once again well above the long-term rate of +6.0%. Further gains increasingly
depend on corporate earnings delivering on elevated expectations, particularly around
AI. Stronger productivity and profits could sustain the rally. If expectations have run
ahead of economic reality, however, the resulting correction could reach far beyond
technology stocks. In a downside scenario in which earnings disappoint and expectations

prove too optimistic, a 25% correction in the S&P 500 could wipe around USD27trn
from US household wealth, equivalent to almost 14% of total net worth, with weaker
confidence and spending pushing the US economy into recession.  In the medium term,
AI should remain an important driver of global wealth but the path will be more volatile.
Fragmentation, persistent inflation and elevated public debt will constrain returns, while
the scale and timing of AI-related productivity gains remain uncertain. Annual financial-

asset growth is therefore expected to settle at around +5–6% over the medium term.
AI offers substantial upside, but translating its technological promise into durable and
broadly shared wealth will take time.

### Page 5

29 September  2026
5
Despite a sharp rise in trade-policy uncertainty and
persistent geopolitical tensions, 2025 saw another
year of resilient global growth. Sweeping US tariff
announcements in April triggered market turbulence
and disrupted trade, yet the US economy remained
comparatively robust, supported by household
consumption and a powerful AI-related investment
boom. Europe continued to wrestle with structural
weakness, even as Germany returned to marginal

growth after two years of recession. In China, weak
domestic demand and the prolonged property-sector
adjustment remained the main constraints.
Inflation continued to ease, although at markedly
different speeds across regions, and with US inflation still
above target. This gave central banks room to loosen
policy: the European Central Bank (ECB) reduced its
deposit rate by 100bps to 2.0%, while the US Federal
Reserve (Fed) waited until September before cutting

by a cumulative 75bps to 3.50–3.75%. Long-term
interest rates remained elevated, however, as investors
demanded compensation for persistent inflation risks,
large fiscal deficits and rising public debt.
Financial markets ultimately looked through the
turbulence and recorded another strong year. After the
sharp sell-off around the April tariff announcements,
easing trade tensions, lower policy rates and continued
enthusiasm for artificial intelligence (AI) drove a strong

recovery. US equities remained powered by AI-related
investment and earnings expectations, while European
markets benefited from a rotation into previously
underperforming countries and sectors. Overall, global
equity markets therefore ended 2025 with substantial
gains extending the strong performance of the previous
two years.
Against this backdrop, private households’ global
financial assets continued to grow at a remarkably
dynamic pace, with an increase of +8.6% in 2025, in line

with the previous year, reaching a record EUR268.4trn
(Figure 1). Relative to global economic activity, financial
assets stood at 288% of GDP at the end of 2025 but
remained below their 2021 peak of 306%.
Adjusted for inflation, the picture is considerably less
impressive: while in nominal terms global financial assets
grew by almost 50% since 2019, real financial assets
grew just 23%, and as of end-2025 register only 5%
above their 2021 level. Part of this year‘s headline record

therefore reflects rising prices rather than genuine new
wealth.
Financial assets:
2025 sets a fresh record

A comparison with pre-Covid real-terms also exposes a
stark regional divide: as of end-2025 Western Europe’s
real financial assets were only 0.5% above their 2019
level, effectively experiencing six years without a

meaningful net gain in real terms. Over the same
period, real financial assets in China rose by 70%, in Asia
excluding Japan and China by 40%, in North America by
21% and in Japan by 13%. Western Europe was therefore
not merely the slowest-growing region, it was the only
one to experience virtual stagnation.
The US remains on top
The enormous stock of global financial assets held by
private households is not distributed evenly across the
world. In fact, it is heavily concentrated in North America

(Figure 2),¹ which alone accounts for roughly half of all
private financial assets worldwide. Remarkably, its share
has changed little over the past two decades, despite
China’s spectacular rise from 3% in 2005 to almost 15%
in 2025. China’s gains came largely at the expense of the
other advanced wealth centers: Western Europe’s share
fell by more than 10pps to 19%, while Japan’s more than
halved to 5%.

North America’s resilience reflects a powerful
combination of scale and performance. Its financial
assets grew by an average of 5.9% annually between
2006 and 2025, broadly in line with the global rate of
6.0%. Western Europe and Japan achieved only 3.7%
and 2.0%, respectively, falling short of global growth by
2.3pps and 4.0pps per year.
In 2025, North American financial assets accelerated
by 9.2%, outperforming the global rate of 8.6% (Figure

3). Emerging markets grew even faster, led by Eastern
Europe, China and Latin America, all at around 11%.
Western Europe lagged markedly at 4.5%, while Japan
recorded growth of 6.5%.

fundamentally change the long-term picture: North

growth. Population growth alone accounts for only part of
the story – per-capita wealth creation runs 0.8pps below

the aggregate global rate in most regions, North America
included. The exception is Japan, where a shrinking
population actually lifts per-capita growth above the
headline figure (Figure 4).

Inflation, by contrast, has a much larger effect than
population growth: it erases more than half of nominal
growth globally in real terms. North America is no
exception, with real per-capita wealth growing at 2.4%

a year against a nominal 5.0%. That 2.4% real rate,
however, is exactly in line with the global average and
comfortably ahead of Western Europe‘s 1.2% – only
Japan and China erode less to inflation than North
America does, and even Japan‘s real per-capita growth
(1.4%) is just narrowly quicker than Western Europe‘s,
not America‘s. Compounded over two decades, this
leaves the purchasing power of North American
financial assets up by a factor of 1.6 – matching the

global multiple, and ahead of the 1.3x recorded in both
Western Europe and Japan. China remains the one
region genuinely out of reach on this measure, with real
per-capita wealth compounding at an astounding 11.9%
a year and purchasing power up almost tenfold over the
same 20 years.
Thanks to its enormous asset base, North America
remains the main driver of global financial wealth
growth in nominal terms, real and per-capita
adjustments notwithstanding. It generated 51.4% of the

worldwide increase in financial assets in 2025. Over the
past two decades, it accounted for 47.5% of the total
increase, compared with 20.1% for China and 14.3%
for Western Europe. In global wealth creation, the US
remains clearly in the driver‘s seat.
North America’s recent outperformance in household
financial-asset growth is a relatively new phenomenon.
In the years following the global financial crisis, US
financial assets frequently grew more slowly than the

global average. The shift began around 2017, when
escalating US–China trade tensions heralded the end of
ever-deeper globalization and the increasingly seamless
integration of emerging economies into the global
division of labor.
This changing environment is also visible in the
narrowing growth gap between emerging and
advanced economies (Figure 5). In the decade leading
up to 2017, emerging-market financial assets on average
grew more than 13pps faster per year. In 2024–2025,

their advantage was only around 3pps. Emerging
markets are still growing faster, but no longer fast
enough to sustain the rapid convergence of the early
2000s. In an increasingly fragmented global economy,
the closing of the global wealth gap has clearly lost
momentum.

While the two top spots have remained unchanged for

Two decades ago, Sweden, Taiwan, Australia and New
Zealand were not yet part of this illustrious circle, which
at that time included Belgium, the UK, Italy and Japan.

Inflation may be well below its post-pandemic peak, but the affordability crisis has not gone away. In the US, 47%² of
registered voters cite the cost of living as the single most important factor in deciding their 2026 midterm vote – a sign
that lower headline inflation has yet to translate into a sustained recovery in household purchasing power.
In fact, the recovery in purchasing power has gone into reverse. After the post-Covid inflation shock, wage growth had

again overtaken inflation, allowing real incomes to recover in both the US and Europe. But in 2026, renewed energy-price
pressures and sticky services inflation have reversed part of that improvement: inflation has risen to around 3.4% in the
US and 3.3% in Europe and could be pushed higher by the energy-price shock triggered by the war in the Middle East.
With nominal labor-income growth failing to keep pace, real labor-income growth has turned negative again for the first

time since the post-pandemic inflation shock.

The squeeze is also highly unequal. Lower-income households spend a higher share of their budgets on necessities and
therefore have less room to adjust. In Europe, food, housing and energy absorb around 61% of disposable income for
those in the bottom income quintile, compared with 45% for the top. In the US, lower-income households devote around

6pps more of their spending to essential goods. As a result, differences in consumption baskets can translate into an
effective inflation rate around 1-2pps higher for poorer households.
The cumulative price shock continues to bite as lower inflation rates do not undo earlier price increases: US food prices,
for example, remain around 36% above 2019 levels. For households already spending most of their income on essentials,
this makes it harder to recover the purchasing power lost during the inflation shock.

The affordability hangover can extend from household budgets to the ballot box. Historical evidence across 365
elections in 18 advanced economies since 1948 finds that a 10pp positive inflation surprise is associated with a 15%

increase in the vote share of extremist, anti-system and populist parties, with the effect particularly pronounced when
real wages fall.³ Inflation does not determine voting behavior, but sharp and unexpected losses of purchasing power can
amplify political discontent.
Bringing inflation down is therefore not the same as restoring affordability. Broad price subsidies can provide quick
relief but are costly, often poorly targeted and can weaken incentives to adjust consumption. Support should instead

focus on households most exposed to essential-price shocks, for example through targeted transfers or tax relief, while
structural policies tackle the underlying drivers of living costs – particularly housing supply, energy costs and competition
in essential services. Wage and benefit systems should meanwhile avoid turning temporary price shocks into persistent
inflation.
The longer-term challenge is to protect households’ capacity to save. When essentials absorb more income, saving is

often the adjustment margin, turning today’s affordability squeeze into tomorrow’s wealth gap. Strengthening access to
long-term saving, funded pensions and financial participation can help prevent an inflation shock from leaving a lasting
scar on household wealth.

Figure 9: Securities set the pace for a third consecutive year
Gross financial assets in 2025 EUR, annual change in %
Markets do the heavy lifting
Financial wealth grows through two channels:
households add new savings, and market movements
change the value of existing portfolios. The second

channel works primarily through securities, especially
equities. Portfolio composition therefore determines how
strongly households benefit from rising markets.
In 2025, securities, including shares, bonds and
investment funds, rose by +12.4%, compared with +5.7%
for bank deposits and +5.0% for insurance and pension
assets (Figure 9). Securities thus led the three major asset
classes for the third consecutive year.
With the sustained stock-market boom delivering

substantial gains, the past three years have been
exceptionally rewarding for invested savers. Between
2023 and 2025, securities grew by an average of +12.1%
annually, more than twice as fast as deposits (+5.2%)
and insurance and pension assets (+5.9%). Their growth
advantage persists over the long term, although it is less
pronounced because capital markets also experience
periodic reversals.
Over the past 20 years, securities grew by an average

of +6.9% annually, compared with +6.0% for total
financial assets. The message is clear: capital-market
participation is increasingly decisive for long-term wealth
accumulation.

Deposits Securities Insurance & pensions
Securities are by far the dominant asset class. At the
end of 2025, they accounted for a record 46.9% of
global financial assets. Despite periodic stock-market
setbacks, the long-term trend has been clear: overall

holdings of shares, investment funds and other securities
increased by 7.4pps compared with 2005. Conversely,
the share of insurance and pension assets declined by a
similar amount to a record low of 24.8%. Bank deposits
meanwhile increased their share slightly to 26.0% (Figure
10).
These global figures mask large differences between
countries and regions, and thus the extent to which
savers benefit from rising securities prices. North
American savers have the greatest capital-market

exposure, with securities accounting for 60.7% of their
portfolios. In the US, the share is even higher, at 61.4%.
This was not always the case: following the global
financial crisis, North America’s securities share fell
below 46%.

### Page 14

Allianz Research
14
Figure 10: Different portfolios, different fortunes.
Gross financial assets, by asset class in 2025 EUR, in % of total gross financial assets
Sources: Eurostat, national central banks, financial supervisory authorities, financial associations and statistical offices, IMF, LSEG, Allianz Research
Securities also play a disproportionately large role in
Latin America, accounting for 45.8% of financial assets.
These holdings, however, are more likely to include

unlisted equity than in North America. Brazil stands out,
with a securities share of 62.1%. In Asia, by contrast,
bank deposits generally play a much larger role: they
account for 54.6% of financial assets in China and 48.8%
in Japan. However, there are important exceptions. In
Australia, many savers participate indirectly in capital
markets through superannuation funds, with insurance
and pension assets accounting for 54.6% of household
portfolios.

Western Europe, meanwhile, is characterized by a more
balanced portfolio structure, with securities significantly
less important than in North America. Here, too, national
differences are substantial: securities account for 54.0%
of financial assets in Italy, while insurance and pension
assets dominate in the Netherlands, with a share of
50.4%.
These different portfolio structures demonstrate the wide
variation in savings behavior around the world. They also

help explain why North American savers have increased
their financial assets broadly in line with global
developments in recent years, unlike their Japanese
and European counterparts. Despite North America’s
already very high level of wealth, its greater exposure
to capital markets has supported continued growth.
Portfolio structure is therefore not merely a reflection of
savings preferences, rather it is an increasingly important
determinant of wealth creation.

Savings losing steam
Savers added less new money in 2025, but markets more
than compensated: 4 out of every 5 euros of additional
financial wealth came from valuation gains rather than

fresh savings.
In 2025, new savings declined by -5.4% to EUR4.1trn
(Figure 11). While this was well below the peaks of the
exceptional years 2020 and 2021, it was still 56% higher
than in 2015. Developments diverged markedly across
regions. New savings in North America fell by -17.5%,
largely because US purchases of securities, insurance
and pension products declined. Nevertheless, the region
still mobilized EUR1.8trn and accounted for around 45%

of total new savings. By contrast, new savings increased
by +34.6% in Japan, +22.2% in Australia and +13.3% in the
eurozone. Overall these flows explain only around one-
fifth of the increase in global assets, with valuation gains
generating the remaining four-fifths.

### Page 15

29 September  2026
15
Another striking feature was the continued recovery in
bank deposits. The savings pool shrank, but the appetite
for liquidity grew: almost one-third of fresh savings
flowed into bank deposits. Inflows had virtually dried up
in 2023, primarily because American savers withdrew
more than EUR500bn and shifted funds into higher-
yielding assets. After recovering in 2024, global deposit
inflows increased by a further +18.1% to EUR1.3trn

in 2025. As a result, deposits attracted 31.7% of new
savings, up from 25.4% in the previous year.
While this is significantly higher than in the previous
two years, it is still significantly lower than in the pre-
pandemic years, when this figure averaged over 40%.
The “rediscovery” of bank deposits is therefore likely due
more to falling interest rates making the opportunity cost
of leaving money in a current account lower, rather than
due to their attractiveness. This is also evident in Japan,

where the delayed interest rate turnaround caused the
proportion of savings in bank deposits to fall to a historic
low of 25%. The recovery was particularly pronounced
in the US, where deposit inflows more than tripled to
EUR321bn. Japan moved in the opposite direction:
although deposit inflows increased slightly in absolute
terms, their share of new savings fell from 28.4% to
23.4%.
Securities suffered another setback after the exceptional
inflows of previous years. Net purchases fell by -20.5% to

EUR1.8trn in 2025, following a decline of -10.9% in 2024.
Even so, securities remained the preferred destination
for new savings, accounting for 42.4% of total flows – a
historically high level – more than any other asset class.
North American savers continued to dominate securities
purchases, but their share fell from almost three-quarters
in 2024 to around two-thirds in 2025. The US alone
accounted for 59% of global purchases. The cooling
of securities flows was therefore largely an American

phenomenon: North American purchases declined by
-29.4% to EUR1.2trn, while inflows remained broadly
stable in Western Europe at EUR333bn and increased
in Japan to EUR79bn. Eastern Europe also recorded a
strong increase, albeit from a much lower base. Global
appetite for securities therefore did not disappear; it
became less concentrated in the US.
Preferences within the securities category shifted again:
households were net sellers of bonds and other debt

securities, with global outflows of EUR63bn compared
with purchases of EUR183bn in 2024. This reversal was
driven by American savers, who sold EUR145bn after
the bond-buying boom of 2022 and 2023. Italian and
Japanese households remained the largest net buyers
among the countries covered, purchasing EUR24bn and
EUR31bn, respectively. However, their paths diverged:
Japanese purchases edged up from EUR29bn in 2024,
while Italian purchases more than halved from EUR55bn.

Shares meanwhile remained in strong demand. Global
purchases reached EUR830bn in 2025, down by -11.3%
from the previous year but still the fourth-highest
amount on record. Once again, the US was the decisive
force, accounting for EUR814bn and therefore virtually
all global net purchases. Elsewhere, activity was broadly
balanced: Western European purchases were close
to zero, while Japanese savers remained net sellers.
Investment funds attracted EUR954bn, a decline of

-11.1% but still the third-highest amount on record. Unlike
direct share purchases, fund inflows were more broadly
distributed: Western European purchases rose by +10.7%
to a record EUR298bn, while North American inflows
fell by -25.0% to EUR500bn. The continued popularity
of Exchange Traded Funds (ETFs) is likely to have
supported these flows.
Inflows into insurance and pension assets declined
by -10.1% to EUR928bn, accounting for only 22.4% of
new savings. However, the global decline was entirely

driven by North America, where inflows fell by -40.8%
to EUR269bn. Western European inflows meanwhile
increased by +25.4% to EUR417bn, while Japan recorded
an even stronger recovery, with inflows rising by +151.9%
to EUR49bn, albeit from a low base. Nevertheless, the
long-term trend remains clear: during the decade before
the pandemic, insurance and pensions attracted an
average of 44% of new savings – almost twice their share
in 2025.
